The candidate may care most about research time and flexibility. The employer may care most about start date and teaching coverage. A purely distributive salary negotiation could miss value.

For example, suppose an employer cannot exceed €70,000 due to salary bands. The candidate wants €76,000 because of living costs and recognition. Instead of ending the conversation, the parties could explore:

A signing bonus. Earlier salary review. Relocation support. Remote work. Reduced teaching in first semester. Research assistant support. Conference budget. Clear promotion timeline. Professional development funding. Flexible start date.

Some of these may be low-cost to the employer but high-value to the candidate. Others may be high-cost and therefore poor trades. The only way to know is to discuss priorities.

A candidate might say:

“I understand the salary band constraint. Could we look at the broader package? Research support, review timing, and flexibility are also important to me.”

An employer might say:

“We have limited flexibility on salary, but more flexibility on start date, professional development, and review schedule. Which of these would matter most?”

This is integrative negotiation because it treats the job offer as a package rather than a single number.

### Organizations

Organizations negotiate constantly: budgets, deadlines, resources, roles, strategy, priorities, credit, responsibility, and change.

Many internal negotiations fail because parties frame issues as departmental competition. Marketing wants more budget. Operations wants more budget. Faculty want autonomy. Administration wants standardization. Sales wants customization. Production wants stability. These positions conflict.

Integrative negotiation asks what interests the positions protect.

Marketing may need budget to generate leads. Operations may need budget to reduce delays. Faculty may need autonomy to protect academic judgment. Administration may need standardization to reduce errors. Sales may need customization to win clients. Production may need stability to deliver quality.

Once interests are explicit, creative options become possible:

Shared infrastructure. Pilot programs. Rotating resources. Service-level agreements. Decision rights by issue. Flexible standards. Contingent funding. Cross-functional teams. Joint metrics. Staged implementation. Trade-offs across time.

For example, a department may resist a new administrative system because it fears loss of autonomy. Administration may insist on standardization because manual processes create errors. An integrative solution might standardize core reporting while allowing local flexibility in workflow. Both interests are addressed.

Internal negotiation also depends on relationship. Unlike one-time market transactions, colleagues must continue working together. A department that claims value aggressively but damages trust may lose long-term cooperation. Integrative negotiation is therefore essential for organizational health.

A useful organizational question is:

What problem is each unit trying to solve, and can we design a system that solves more than one problem at once?

### Public policy

Public policy negotiations often involve multiple stakeholders with different values, risks, and constraints. Integrative negotiation can help move beyond symbolic conflict.

Consider climate policy. One side may emphasize emissions reduction; another side may emphasize jobs, affordability, energy security, or regional fairness. A purely positional debate becomes “regulation versus freedom” or “environment versus economy.” An integrative approach asks how policies can reduce emissions while addressing transition costs: retraining, regional investment, technological innovation, compensation, phased timelines, and energy reliability.

Consider urban planning. Residents may oppose new housing because of traffic, school capacity, neighborhood identity, or green space. Developers may want density. City governments may want affordability. An integrative agreement may include transit investment, design standards, affordable units, green space, school funding, and phased development.

Consider healthcare. Patients want access and dignity. Providers want manageable workload. Governments want cost control. Insurers want predictability. Integrative solutions may involve triage redesign, digital tools, preventive care, payment reform, and shared decision-making.

Policy negotiation is difficult because interests are often public, moral, and identity-laden. Not everything can be traded. Some values are sacred or rights-based. Still, many policy conflicts include practical dimensions that can be structured creatively.

The risk is that integrative language can be used to pressure weaker parties into compromise. Ethical integration must respect rights, dignity, and power imbalances. Creating value should not mean asking vulnerable groups to trade away basic protections.

A useful policy question is:

Which parts of this conflict are true value conflicts, and which parts are design problems?

The first may require democratic choice. The second may allow integrative design.

## Ethics, process, and implementation

### Ethics of value creation

Integrative negotiation sounds morally attractive because it aims for mutual gain. But it also has ethical risks.

First, value creation can become a cover for manipulation. A powerful party may say, “Let us create value,” while using the conversation to extract information and then claim most of the gains.

Second, integrative language can pressure people to be “reasonable” when they should protect themselves. A party with weak power may be told to collaborate while the stronger party controls the terms.

Third, contingent contracts can create perverse incentives. If payment depends on performance metrics, parties may manipulate metrics. If penalties are too severe, parties may hide problems. If bonuses reward speed, quality may suffer.

Fourth, MESOs can be designed as deceptive choice architecture. A negotiator may present options that appear flexible but all favor one side, or include decoy options to steer the other party.

Fifth, information sharing can be abused. If one party reveals interests in good faith and the other exploits them, trust is violated.

Ethical integrative negotiation requires:

Truthful representation. Reciprocal information sharing. Respect for BATNAs and rights. Attention to power asymmetry. No hidden traps in complex packages. Clear measurement in contingent terms. No perverse incentives. Implementation fairness. Voluntary agreement.

The ethical test is not only whether both parties signed. It is whether the process allowed informed, voluntary, and respectful agreement.

A useful question is:

Does this value-creating proposal make both parties genuinely better off, or does it merely make exploitation look cooperative?

The language of win-win should be earned, not assumed.

### An integrative process

Integrative negotiation can be approached as a structured process.

First, prepare separately. Identify issues, interests, priorities, BATNA, reservation value, target, standards, and possible trades.

Second, create a constructive process. Agree to discuss multiple issues, explore interests, and avoid premature commitment.

Third, exchange information gradually. Share interests and priorities, not hard limits. Ask diagnostic questions. Listen for constraints and hidden values.

Fourth, identify differences. Look for differences in valuation, cost, risk tolerance, time preference, beliefs, capabilities, and stakeholder needs.

Fifth, construct packages. Avoid negotiating issues one by one. Build offers that combine issues.

Sixth, use MESOs when useful. Present multiple packages equivalent to you but different in structure. Learn from the other side’s preferences.

Seventh, consider contingent contracts when beliefs differ. Let future outcomes determine part of the agreement if outcomes can be measured and incentives remain aligned.

Eighth, claim value fairly. Once value is created, negotiate division using BATNAs, standards, fairness, and relationship considerations.

Ninth, test for Pareto improvements. Ask whether any changes could make one party better off without hurting the other.

Tenth, plan implementation. Clarify responsibilities, timing, metrics, review, and dispute resolution.

Eleventh, consider post-settlement settlement. After reaching agreement, ask whether both parties want to search safely for improvements.

A simple integrative checklist:

What are all the issues? What are my interests? What are their interests? Which issues matter most to me? Which issues matter most to them? Where do we differ? What can I give that costs me little and helps them a lot? What can they give that costs them little and helps me a lot? Could a package create more value than issue-by-issue bargaining? Could a contingent term resolve different beliefs? Is the agreement Pareto efficient? Is the agreement fair enough to implement? Have we protected our BATNA and ethics?

This process turns negotiation from a contest into a design problem.

### Worked example: supplier contract

A manufacturing firm is negotiating with a component supplier. The buyer wants a low price, flexible delivery, high quality, and short lead time. The supplier wants higher price, predictable volume, longer contract length, and earlier payment.

A distributive negotiation over unit price may become difficult. The buyer wants €10 per unit. The supplier wants €12. They argue and settle at €11. But this compromise may miss value.

An integrative negotiation begins by identifying interests.

The buyer’s real interests:

Keep production costs low.

Avoid stockouts.

Maintain quality.

Preserve flexibility because demand is uncertain.

Reduce cash tied up in inventory.

The supplier’s real interests:

Protect margin.

Predict production volume.

Reduce last-minute changes.

Improve cash flow.

Justify investment in tooling.

The parties identify differences. The supplier values predictable volume more than the buyer realized. The buyer values flexibility more than the supplier realized. The supplier can provide higher quality at modest cost if volume is stable. The buyer can pay earlier for part of the order if price is lower.

A possible integrative package:

Base price of €10.80 per unit.

Two-year contract with minimum annual volume.

Flexible monthly delivery within a defined band.

Early payment discount for part of the volume.

Quality bonus if defect rates stay below threshold.

Shared forecast updates every month.

Option to increase volume at pre-agreed price if demand rises.

This package may be better for both than simply splitting price. The supplier gets predictability, cash flow, and potential bonus. The buyer gets flexibility, quality, and a price below the supplier’s initial demand.

The lesson is that price was not the whole problem. Price was the visible conflict. Value was hidden in volume, timing, risk, quality, and information.

### Worked example: academic workload

Two faculty members are negotiating responsibilities for a new master’s course. Both initially say they cannot take more work. The department head considers splitting tasks equally. But equal division may not fit interests or capabilities.

Faculty member A enjoys lectures and conceptual framing but dislikes grading and administrative coordination. Faculty member B prefers smaller interactive sessions, assessment design, and student feedback but dislikes large lectures. A has heavy research travel in October. B has heavy committee work in December.

A compromise might split lectures, grading, and administration equally. An integrative agreement might allocate tasks by strengths and timing:

A gives more large lectures early in the semester.

B designs assessments and leads workshops.

A prepares conceptual lecture notes.

B manages feedback rubrics.

Administrative tasks are reduced through templates.

Grading is divided based on timing availability.

Both agree on shared office hours before exams.

This agreement may create more value because it uses differences in preferences, capabilities, and calendars. It is not equal in every task, but it may be fairer and more efficient overall.

This example shows that integrative negotiation is not only for business deals. It is useful in everyday organizational life.

### From agreement to architecture

This chapter closes the negotiation part of the book. Negotiation is a joint decision process. It requires rational preparation, strategic thinking, social understanding, persuasion, storytelling, communication, and ethical judgment. Distributive negotiation helps parties avoid bad deals and claim value. Integrative negotiation helps parties discover better deals and create value.

Chapter 35 turns from individual agreements to recurring decision systems. Negotiation designs a better conversation and agreement; decision hygiene designs the agendas, roles, forecasts, checks, and feedback through which future decisions will be made.

*Table 34.1  A multi-issue priority map*

| Issue | My position | My interest | My priority | Their likely position | Their likely interest | Their likely priority |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Price | Lower | Budget constraint | High | Higher | Margin/reputation | High |
| Delivery | Flexible | Planning certainty | Medium | Earlier | Capacity utilization | Low/Medium |
| Payment timing | Later | Cash flow | High | Earlier | Cash flow | Medium |
| Service level | High | Reliability | High | Standard | Cost control | Medium |
| Contract length | Flexible | Optionality | Low | Longer | Predictability | High |

## Key ideas

- Better agreements can reveal priorities through multiple packages, convert forecast disagreement into contingent terms, stage trust, and reopen an initial settlement only when both parties can improve without risk.

MESOs and contingent agreements help reveal priorities and manage differences in beliefs, provided outcomes are measurable and incentives are well aligned (Raiffa et al., 2002; Thompson, 2015).

- Design multiple equivalent simultaneous offers.
- Use contingent contracts when beliefs differ and performance can be measured.
- Manage disclosure through staged reciprocity and verification.

## Study and practice

1. Design multiple equivalent simultaneous offers?

2. How would you use contingent contracts when beliefs differ and performance can be measured in practice?

3. Manage disclosure through staged reciprocity and verification?

# Part 7. Better Decision Systems

How to make good judgment repeatable through decision hygiene, structured disagreement, and learning.


# Chapter 35. Decision Hygiene

*Notice, test, ask, design, and learn*

<aside class="callout core-idea"><div class="callout-title">CORE IDEA</div><div class="callout-body">Better judgment is less a personality trait than a property of a well-designed process: independent evidence, explicit assumptions, structured disagreement, meaningful alternatives, ethical influence, and feedback protected from hindsight.</div></aside>

## Learning goals

- Design a decision process before debating the answer.
- Protect independent judgment and constructive dissent.
- Use environments, messages, conversations, and agreements as design objects.
- Debrief process quality separately from outcome.

<aside class="callout the-final-standard"><div class="callout-title">THE FINAL STANDARD</div><div class="callout-body">A useful intervention should remain defensible if the people affected understand how it works, whose objective it serves, what evidence supports it, and how they can refuse or revise it.</div></aside>

By the end of this book, a familiar temptation appears: make a list of biases, promise to remember them, and trust the improved self to perform better next time. That is rarely enough. A bias label does not automatically change a meeting. Knowledge about anchors does not prevent an anchor from entering. Knowing that people conform does not make dissent socially safe. Knowing one should ask about interests does not make the question appear under pressure.

Decision hygiene treats judgment as a system property. The central question is not only whether an individual is intelligent or well intentioned. It is whether the process makes relevant evidence visible, protects independent thought, separates prediction from valuation, creates useful alternatives, records uncertainty, and produces feedback that can be learned from.

## Before the decision: improve the input

Start by defining the decision, the decision-maker, the deadline, and the real constraints. Generate alternatives before comparing them. Include doing nothing, delaying, piloting, negotiating, combining options, and changing the problem. Then make the best feasible alternative forgone visible. The sentence "By choosing this, we give up..." is a practical defense against invisible opportunity cost.

Decision hygiene improves judgment by changing the process around the judge: independent estimates, structured aggregation, premortems, calibration, and after-action review (Klein, 2007; Milkman et al., 2009; Soll et al., 2015; Kahneman et al., 2021).

Use the outside view before the inside story becomes emotionally complete. Identify a reference class and ask what usually happens. Collect independent estimates before discussion so that hierarchy, charisma, and early anchors do not manufacture agreement. Predefine evaluation criteria when hiring, selecting projects, or judging proposals. A rubric cannot eliminate judgment, but it can stop the first global impression from silently deciding every criterion.

## During the decision: structure disagreement

Separate prediction from valuation. Ask first what each person believes will happen and what evidence supports that belief. Ask separately what outcomes matter, which trade-offs are acceptable, and whose welfare or rights count. Many arguments become clearer when "too risky" is decomposed into probability and consequence.

Assign dissent a role rather than waiting for courage. Use a premortem: imagine that the decision failed and list plausible reasons. Ask one person to construct the strongest case against the emerging preference. Protect the dissenter from becoming the problem. The purpose is not permanent opposition; it is to expose information before commitment makes it expensive to hear.

## Design choices, not just advice

When behavior repeats, redesign the environment. Change defaults, friction, prompts, timing, information format, social visibility, and feedback. Make desired actions easier at the moment they must occur and make harmful impulsive actions harder. Ethical choice architecture preserves meaningful exit and serves goals that the affected person could endorse under informed reflection.

In persuasion, begin with the audience model rather than the speaker's facts. Identify the current story, the barrier to updating, the protected identity, and the evidence that would be treated as credible. In communication, ask before assuming and make interpretation testable. In negotiation, improve the BATNA, map interests and priorities, and prepare packages before the pressure of the table.

## After the outcome: learn without worshipping luck

Review the process using what was knowable at the time. A good decision can produce a bad outcome, and a poor process can get lucky. Compare forecasts with outcomes, not stories with feelings. Ask which assumptions failed, which information was missing, which values changed, and which parts of the process should remain even though the outcome was disappointing.

Do not update from one noisy result more than the evidence warrants. Regression to the mean, random variation, and selective feedback can create false lessons. Calibrate over many predictions. Reward accurate uncertainty and timely revision, not only confident success.

## The decision-improvement cycle: notice, test, ask, design, learn

<figure class="book-figure"><img src="figures/decision-improvement-cycle.png" alt="Circular five-step cycle: notice, test, ask, design, and learn, around decision hygiene." /><figcaption>Figure 35.1. Notice, test, ask, design, and learn form a repeatable cycle for improving decisions, persuasion, communication, and negotiation.</figcaption></figure>

The course can be compressed into five verbs. Notice what attention selected and what it excluded. Test the model with base rates, counterevidence, experiments, and alternative explanations. Ask other people for their perspective, interests, constraints, and meaning. Design the environment, message, conversation, or agreement so that good action is easier. Learn from a record that existed before the outcome.

Decision, persuasion, communication, and negotiation are not separate skills. They are different forms of model management. In each case, we are trying to understand how a mind represents a situation, what it expects, what it values, which actions appear possible, and what feedback will change the next cycle. The deepest practical lesson is therefore not "think harder." It is "build a process in which better thinking has a chance to occur."

*Table 35.1  The integrated decision audit*

| Move | Core question | Typical tool |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Notice | What entered attention, and what stayed invisible? | Attention audit; option generation |
| Test | What would disconfirm the current model? | Base rate; premortem; pilot; alternative explanation |
| Ask | Whose perspective, interest, or constraint is missing? | Perspective-getting; diagnostic questions |
| Design | How can the environment or process make better action easier? | Criteria; defaults; friction; packages; role design |
| Learn | What did we predict, what happened, and what was luck? | Decision journal; calibration; process debrief |

## Key ideas

- Better judgment is less a personality trait than a property of a well-designed process: independent evidence, explicit assumptions, structured disagreement, meaningful alternatives, ethical influence, and feedback protected from hindsight.
- Design a decision process before debating the answer.
- Protect independent judgment and constructive dissent.
- Use environments, messages, conversations, and agreements as design objects.

## Study and practice

1. Design a decision process before debating the answer?

2. Protect independent judgment and constructive dissent?

3. How would you use environments, messages, conversations, and agreements as design objects in practice?

# Appendix A. Portable Course Tools

<aside class="callout one-page-decision-journal"><div class="callout-title">ONE-PAGE DECISION JOURNAL</div><div class="callout-body">Decision and deadline; alternatives considered; best feasible alternative forgone; predictions with probabilities; values and trade-offs; assumptions; emotional and social context; review date. After the outcome: compare forecast with result, separate process from luck, and state what should and should not change.</div></aside>

<aside class="callout attention-audit"><div class="callout-title">ATTENTION AUDIT</div><div class="callout-body">What captured attention? What was missing? Which question, role, metric, order, device, or time pressure made that pattern likely? What design would make the neglected variable visible?</div></aside>

<aside class="callout behavior-redesign-canvas"><div class="callout-title">BEHAVIOR REDESIGN CANVAS</div><div class="callout-body">Target behavior; cue ecology; urge or local problem; current response; immediate reward or relief; smallest replacement; friction change; prompt; immediate reinforcement; measurement; recovery plan; ethical check.</div></aside>

<aside class="callout persuasion-canvas"><div class="callout-title">PERSUASION CANVAS</div><div class="callout-body">Audience current model; protected identity; barrier to change; meaningful mismatch; new causal model; credible evidence; appropriate emotion; source credibility; agency-preserving action; uncertainty and counterargument.</div></aside>

<aside class="callout layered-listening"><div class="callout-title">LAYERED LISTENING</div><div class="callout-body">WHAT happened? HOW did it feel or unfold? WHY does it matter? WHO is implicated in identity or relationship? WHEN is the timing or history relevant? ACTION: what does the person want now - understanding, advice, decision, repair, or commitment?</div></aside>

<aside class="callout negotiation-preparation"><div class="callout-title">NEGOTIATION PREPARATION</div><div class="callout-body">BATNA and how to improve it; reservation value; target; opening offer; objective standards; issues and priorities; estimate of their interests and alternatives; questions; packages; concession plan; ethical boundaries; implementation and review.</div></aside>
